Peace be with the soul of that charitable and courteous author

the ingenious way of miscellaneous writing ! It must be owned

of wit lias been more plcntifiil, and the labourers more in

' tioners in the writing art "that as easy as it is to conceive 

wit, 'tis the hardest thing imaginable to be dehvered of it, 

upon certain terms." Nothing could be more severe or rigid 

than the conditions formerly prescribed to writers, when 

criticism took place, and regularity and order were thought 

I essential in a treatise. The notion of a genuine work, a 

I legitimate and just piece, has certainly been the occasion of 

I great timidity and backwardness among the adventurers in wit ; 

and the imposition of such strict laws and rules of composition 

has sat heavy on the free spirits and forward geniuses of mankind. 

'Twas a yoke, it seems, which our forefathers bore, but which, 

for our parts, we have generously thrown off. In effect, the 

invidious distinctions of bastanly and legitimacy being at length 

removed, the natural and lawful issue of the brain cornea with

To explain the better how this revolution in letters has been

adjustment of the various figui-es and devices as may, in the 
whole, create a kind of harmony to the eye. According to this 
method each piece must be in reality an original. For to 
copy what has gone before can be of no use. 'ITie fraud would 
easily be perceived. On the other side, to work oHginally, and 
in a manner create each time anew, must be a matter of press- 
ing weight, and fitted to the strength and ca]wu:ity of none 
l)esides the choicest workmen. 

A manner therefore is invented to confound this simplicity 
and conformity of design ; patchwork is substituted ; cuttings 
and shreds of learning, with various fragments and points of wit, 
are drawn together Euid tacked in any fantastic form. If they 
chance to cast a lustre and spread a sort of sprightly glare, the 
miscellany is approved and the complex form and texture of 
the work admii-ed. The eye, which before was to be won by 
p^ularity, and had kept true to measure and strict proportion, 
is by this means pleasingly drawn aside to commit a kind of

shapes of things. Custom in the meanwhile has not only

room of the graceful and the beautiful. Justness and accuracy 
of thought are set aside as too constraining and of too painful

merce of gallantry and modera wit.

Whethee, in fact, there be any real enchantment, any infltience 
of stars, any power of demons or of foreign natures over our 

, minds, is thought questionable by many. Some there are 
who assert the negative, and endeavour to solve the appearances 
of thii^ kind by the natural operation of our passions and the 
common course of outward things. For my own part, I cannot 
but at this present apprehend a kind of enchantment or magic 
in that which we call enthusiasm ; since I find that, (having 
touched shghtly on this subject, I cannot so easily part with it 
at pleasure. 

After having made some cursory reflections on our author's 
letter,* I thought I might have sufficiently acquitted myself on 
this head, till passing to his next treatise I found myself still 
farther engaged, I peix^ived plainly that I had as yet scarce 
entered into our author's humour, or felt anything of that 
paadon which, as he informs us, is so easily communicable

first reflections I found naturally rising in me upon second

I have heard indeed that the very reading of treatises and 
accounts of melancholy has been apt to generate that jjassion 
in the over-diligent and attentive reader. And this, perhaps, 
may have been the reason why our author himself (as he seems 
to intimate towards the conclusion of his first letter) cared not

the precise definition of enthusiasm. Uliis, however, we may, 
with our author, pi-esume to infer from the coolest of all studies, 
even from criticism itself (of which we have been lately treating), 
" that there is a power in numbers, harmony, proportion, and 
beauty of every kind, which naturally captivates the heart, and 
raises the imagination to an opinion or conceit of something'l 
majestic and divine." 

Whatever this subject may be in itself, we cannot help 
being transported with the thought of it. It inspires us with 
something more than oixiinary, and raises us above ourselves. 
Without this imagination or conceit the world would be but ■ 
dull circumstance, and life a sorry {mstinie. Scarce could we bed 
said to live. The animal functions might in their course befl 
carried on; but nothing fuilher sought for or regarded. The^^ 
gallant sentiments, the elegant fancies, the belles poMiona wbich.'l 
have, all of them, this beauty in view, would be set aside, and^l 
leave us probably no other employment than that of satisfyinpl 
our coarsest appetites at the cheapest rate, in order to the attain- P 
ment of a supine state of indolence and inactivity.

Tis indeed peculiar to the genius of that cool philosophy

of things, must in appearance be set in a fair way of being

^^1 in the account of these philosophers be rei^koned among those

so in that principal subject, the ba^is and foundation of this 

conceit. Now if the subject itself be not in nature, neither the 

idea nor the passion grounded on it can be properly esteemed 

natural; and thus all admiration ceases, and enthusiasm is at 

an end. But if there be naturally such a passion, 'tis evident 

that religion itself is of the kind, and must be therefore natural 

to man.

There were, besides, in Egypt some natural causes of super- 
stition, beyond those which were common to other regions. 
This nation might well abound in prodigies, when even their 
country and soil itself was a kind of prodigy in nature. Their 
solitary idle life whilst shut up in their houses by the regular 
inundations of the Nile ; the unwholesome vapours arising from 
the new mud and slimy relicts of their river exposed to the 
hot suns; their various met«ors and phenomena, with the long 
vacancy they had to observe and comment on them ; the 
necessity, withal, which, on the account of their navigation 
and the measure of their yearly drowned lands, com])elled them 
to promote the studies of astronomy and other sciences, of 
which their priesthood coutd make good advantages — all these 
may be reckoned perhaps as additional causes of the immense 
growth of superstition, and the enormous increase of the pricB 
hood in this fertile land.

soothsayers, prophets, or such who gain their livelihood i 
receive advantages by officiating in religious affairs." For>|

with full fonlinL-nce and trust, to the opinions' by law estab-

undei^o it. 

To say truth, I have often wondered to find such a disturb- 
ance raiacd about the simple name of sceptic' Tis certain 
that, in ita original and plain signification, the word imports no 
more than tiaroly " tliat state or frame of mind in which every 

me remains on every subject of which he is not certain," He 
who is certain, or presumes to say he knowh is in that particular, 
whether he be mistaken or in the right, a liogmatist. Between 
these two states or situations of mind there can be no medium. 
For he who says *' that he believes for certain, or is o-tsured of 
what be believes," either speaks ridiculously or says in effect 

that he believes strongly, but is not sure." So that whoever is 
not conscious of revelation, nor has certain knowledge of any 
miracle or sign, can be no more than .sceptic in the case; and 
the best Christian in the world, who being destitute of the 
means of certainty depends only on history and tradition for 
his belief in these particulars, is at best but a sceptic Christian. 
He has no more tlian a nicely critical ' historical faith, subject 
to various speculations, and a tliousand different criticisms of 
languages and literature. 

This he will naturally find to be the cose if he attempts to 
search into originals in order to be his own judge, and proceed 
on the bottom of his own discernment and understanding, If. 
ID the other hand, he is no critic, nor competently learned in 

lese originals, 'tis plain he can have no original judgment of 
'liis own, but must rely still on the opinion of those who have 
opportunity to examine :«uch matters, and whom he takes to 
be the unbiassed and disinterested judges of these religious 
narratives. His faith is not in ancient facts or persons, nor in

The holy founders and inspired authors of our religion 
required not, it seems, so strict an assent, or such implicit faith 
in behalf of their original writings and revelations, a.s later un- 
inspired doctors, without the help of divine testimony or any 
miracle on their side, have required in behalf of their own 
comments and interpretations. 'Hie earliest and worst of 
heretics, 'tis said, were those called Gnastics, who took their 
name from an audaeiou.s pretence to certain knowledge and 
comprehension of the greatest mysteries of faith. If the most 
dangerous state of opinion was this dogmatical and presump- 
tuous sort, the safest, in all likelibootl, must be the sceptical and 
modest. 

There is nothing more evident than that our holy religion, 
in its original constitution, was set so far apart from all 
philosophy or refined speculation, that it seemed in a manner 
diametrically opposed to it, A nmii might have been not only 
a sceptic in all the controverted points of the academies or 
Hcbools of learning, but even a perfect stranger to all of this 
kind : and yet complete in his religion, faith, and worship.

Were two travellers agreed to tell their story lieparate in 
public, the one being a man of sincerity, but positive and 
dogmatical; the other Jess sincere, but easy wnd good-liiniioured; 
tliough it happened that the accounts of this latter gentleman

strongly asserted relations and vehement narratives of the other 
fiei-ce defender of the truth. 

'I'hat good -humour is a chief cause of compliance or 
acquiescence in matters of faitli, may be proved from the very 

I spirit of those whom we commonly call critics. Tis a known 
prevention against th« gentlemen of this character, " that they 
are generally ill-humoured and splenetic." The world will 
needs have it that their spleen disturbs them. And I must 
confess I think the world in general to be so far right in this 
conceit, that though all critics perhaps are not necessarily 
gplenetic, all splenetic people (whether naturally such, or made 
•o by ill-usage) have a necessary pro|)ensity to criticism and 
satire. When men are easy in themselves they let others 
remain so, and can i-eadily comply with what seems plausible, 
L and is thought conducing to the quiet or good corresjiondenee 
of mankind. They study to raise no difficulties or doubts. 
And in religious affairs 'tis seldom tliat they are known forward 
to entertain ill thoughts or surmises, whilst they are unmolested. 
But if disturbed by groundless arraignments and suspicions, by 
unnecessary invectives and bitter declamations, and by a con- 

• tentious quarrelsome aspect of religion, they naturally turn 
critics, and begin to question everything. The spirit of satire 
rises with the ill mood ; and the chief passion of men ttius 
diseased and thrown out of good humour is to find fault, 
censure, unravel, confound, and leave nothing without exception 
d controversy. 

These are tlie sceptics or scrupidists, against whom there is 
ch a clamour raised. "Tis evident in the meanwhile that 
e very clamour itself, joined with the usual menaces and show

look with jt'alousy ou every unconformable opinion, and is sure

Come we now (as authentic rbeturicians express themselves) 
to our second bead, which we should again subdivide into firsts 
and seconds but that this manner of carving is of late days 
grown much out of fashion. 

Twas the custom of our ancestors, perhaps as long since as 
the days of our hospitable King Arthur, to have nothing served 
at table but what was entire and substantial. Twas a whole 
boar or solid ox which made the feast. The figure of the 
animal was preserved entire, and the dissection made in form 
by the appointed carver, a man of might as well as profound 
craft and notable dexterity, who was seen erect, with goodly 
mien and action, displaying headfi and members, dividing 
according to art, and distributing his subject matter into 
proper parts, suitable to the stomachs of those lie served. In 
latter days 'tis become the fashion to eat with less ceremony 
and method. Every one chooses to carve for himself. The 
learned manner of dissection is out of request ; and a certain 
method of cookery has been introduced, by which the anatomical 
science of the table is entirely set aside. Ragouts and fricassees 
are the reigning dishes, in which everything is so dismembered 
and thrown out of all order and form that no part of the mass 
can properly be divided, or distinguished from another. 

Fashion is indeed a powerfiil mistress, and by her single 
authority has so far degraded the carving method and use of 
VOL. 11 225 Q 



shaftesbuhys chakacteristicsI 

solids, even in discourse and writing, that our rcligiotis pastors 
themselves have many of them changed their manner of dis- 
tributing to us their spiritual food. They have quitted tlieir 
substantial service and unifonn diviNton into parts and under- 
parts, and in order to become fashionable, they have run into 
the more savoury way of learned ragout and medley. Tis 
unbred rustic orator alone who presents his clownish audi) 
with a divisible discourse. The elegant court divine exhorta 
miscellany, and is ashamed to bring his twos and threes before 
fashionable assembly. 

Should I therefore, as a mere mi seel Ian an an or essay writer, 
forgetting what 1 had premised, be found to drop a head and 
lose the connecting thread of my present discourse, the case 
perhaps would not be so preposteious. For fear, however, lest 
I should be charged for Iwiug woi-se than my word, I shall 
endeavoiu- to satisfy my reader by pursuing my method pro- 
posed, if pera{lventure he can call to mind what that method 
was. Or if he cannot, the matter is not so very impoi'tont ' 
he may safely pursue his reading without further trouble. 

To proceed, therefore. Whatever means or methods 
be employed at any time in maintaining or propagating 
religious belief already current and established, 'tis evii' 
that the first beginnings must have been founded in 
natural complacency and good humour which inclines to 
and confidence in mankind. Terrors alone, though accom] 
with miracles and prodigies of whatever kind, are not ca] 
of raising that sincere faith and absolute reliance which 
required in favour of the divinely authorised instructor and 
spiritual chief. The affection and love which procures a true 
adherence to the new religious foundation must depend cithec 
on a real or counterfeit goodness* in the religious fount 
Whatever ambitious spirit may inspire him, whatever 
zeal or persecuting principle may lie in reserve ready to dii

boy, hoping to hide out of the way. But his Tutor had good 
eyes and a long reach. He overtook him at sea, where a 
storm was ready prepared for his exercise, and a fish's belly for 
his lodging. The renegade found himself in harder durance 
tlian any at land. He was sufficiently mortified. He grew 
good, prayed, moralised, and spoke mightily against tying 
vanities.^

religion carry constantly one and the same face, and were it

'ITiis, however, I may presume to assert, that there are un-

are humorous and pleasant in themselves, and that the sadder 
representations of it are many times so over-sad and dismal, 
that they are apt to excite a very contrary passion to what is 
intended by the representers.

as the lower kind. Cockle-shell tibounds with each. Many 
things exterior and without oui-selves, of no relation to our 
real interests or to those of society and mankind, are diligently 
investigated; Natures remotest operations, deepest mysteries 
and most difficult phenomena discussed and whimsically ex- 
plained ; hypotheses and fantastic systems erected, a universe 
anatomised, and by some notable scheme ' so sohed and reduced 
as to a]]]iear an easy knai:k or secret to those who have the clue. 
Creation itself can, upon occasion, be exhibited ; transmutations, 
projections, and other philosophical arcana, such as in the 
corjwreal world can accomplish all tilings ; whilst in tlie intel- 
lectual a set frame of metaphysical phrases and distinctions can 

e to solve whatever difficulties may be propounded either 
logics, ethics, or any real science of whatever kind. 

It appeal's from hence that the defects of philosophy and 
those of virtuosoship are of the same nature. Nothing can be 
more dangerous than a wrong choice or misapplication in these 
oflairs. But as ridiculous as these studies are rendered by their 
;less managers, it appears, however, that each of them are, 

their nature, essential to the character of a fine gentleman 
and man of sense. 

To philosophise, in a just signification, is but to carry 

good-breeding a step higher. For the accomplishment of 

breeding is, to learn whatever is decent in company or beautiful 

arts; and the sum of philosophy is, to learn what is just in 

iety and beautiful in Nature and the oi-der of the world. 

Tis not wit merely, but a temper which must form the well- 
bred man. In the same manner, 'tis not a head merely, but a 
heart and resolution which must coin|]lL'tf the real philosopher. 
Doth chaiacters aim at what is excellent, aspire to a just taste, 
and carry in view the model of what is lieautiful and becoming. 
Accordingly, the res(>ective conduct and ilistinct manners of

to the strictest interest of mankind and society ; tlie one accord-

Whether each of these offices or social parts are in them-

is handsome ; for whatcier he jiractises in this kind,' he

dei-ent, just, and amiable perfects the character of the gentle- 
man and the philosopher. jVnd the study of such a taste or 
relish will, as we suppose, be ever the great employment and 
concern of him who covets as well to be wise and good as 
agreeable and polite.

SHAFTESBURY'S C H ARACTERISTICS 

as we would wish. For who would not rejoice to be always 
equal and consonant to himself, and have constantly that opinion 
of things which is natural and proportionable ? But who dares 
search opinion to the bottom, or call in question his early and 
prepossessing taste ? Who is so just to himself as to recall his 
fancy from the power of fashion and education to that of reason ? 
Could we, however, be thus courageous, we should soon settle in 
ourselves such an opinion of good as would secure to us an in- 
variable, agreeable, and just taste in life and manners. 

Thus have I endeavoured to tread in my author^s steps, and 
prepare the reader for the serious and downright philosophy 
which even in this ^ last commented treatise, our author keeps 
still as a mystery and dai'es not formally profess. His pretence 
has been to advise authors and polish styles, but his aim has 
been to correct mannei*s and regular lives. He has afTectcd 
soliloquy, as pretending only to censure himself, but he has taken 
occasion to bring others into his company and make bold with 
pei*sonages and characters of no inferior rank. He has given 
scope enough to raillery and humour, and has intrenched very 
largely on the province of us miscellanarian writers. But the 
reader is ' now about to see him in a new aspect, '* a formal and 
professed philosopher, a system-writer, a dogmatist and ex- 
pounder.*" Habes confitentem i-eum. 

So to his philosophy I commit him. lliough, according as 
my genius at present disposition will })ermit, I intend still to 
accompany him at a distance, keep him in sight, and convoy him, 
the best I am able, through the dangerous seas he is about to pass.

The Mahometan dorgy spcm to have a diHerent policy. 

icy boldly rest the foundation of their religion on a book ; 
such a one as (according to their pretension) is not only perfect, 
but iiumitable. Were a real man of letters and a just critic 
permitted to examine this scripture by the known rules of art, 
he would soon perhaps refute this plea. But so barbarous is 
■the accompanying policy and temper of these Eastern religion- 
that they discourage and in eftect extinguish all ti-uo 
learning, science, and the politer arts, in company with the 
ancient authors and languages, which they set aside ; and by 
Ihis infallible method leave their sacred writ the sole standard 
of literate performance. For being compared to nothing besides 
ibielf, or what is of an inferior kind, it must undoubtedly be 
thought incom])ai'able. 

Twill be yielded, surely, to the honour of the Christian 
world, that their faith (especially that of the Protestant 
churches) stands on a more generous foundation. 'ITicy not 
only allow comparison of authors, but are content to derive 
their proofs of the validity of their sacred record and revelation 
i-\en fi-oni those autliors called profane; an being well apprised, 
(iux»rding to the maxim of our Divine Master ') " that in what 
we bear witness only to ourselves, our witness cannot be 
established as a truth." So that, there being at present no 
immediate testimony of niii^icle or sign in l>ehalf of Holy Writ, 
there being in its own particular composition or style 

:bing miraculous or self-convincing ; if the collateral testi- 
tnony of other ancient records, historians, and foreign authors 
were destroyed or wholly lost, there would be less argument or 
plea remaining against that natural suspicion of those who are 
called sceptical, " tliat the holy records themselves were no 
other than the pure invention or artificial compilement of an 
interested party in behalf of the richest corporation and most 
imfitable monopoly which could be erected in the world." 

Thus, in i-eality, the interest of our pious clergy is nec'cssarily

^^k Twas to this art, so well understood and practised heretofore,

to refute the heathens, Jews, sectarians, heretics, and other 
enemies or opposers of our primitive and ancient faith.

to back their spiritual pretences."

of commission to commission, and this din of hell, anathemas, 

damnations raised everywhere by one religions party against 

ither.

the apologist, afterwards excusing this boldness of the people 
and soothing the incensed priests with fairer words, says to 
them, on a foot of moderation, which he presumes to be their 
character : — 

You with such temper their intemperance bear. 

To show your solid science does rely 
So on itself as you no trial fear : 

For arts are weak that are of sceptics shy.^ 

The poet, it seems, never dreamt of a time when the verj* 
countenance of moderation should be out of fashion with the 
gentlemen of this order, and the word itself exploded as 
unworthy of their profession. And, indeed, so far are they at 
present from bearing with any sceptic or inquirer, ever so 
modest or discreet, that to hear an argument on a contrarj* side 
to theii's, or read whatever may be writ in answer to their 
particular assertions, is made the highest crime. Whilst they 
have among themselves such differences and sharp debates about 
their heavenly commission, and are even in one and the same 
community or establishment divided into different sects and 
headships, they will allow no particular survey or insjxjction 
into the foundations of their controverted title. Thev would 
have us inferior passive mortals, amazed as we are, and behold- 
ing with astonishment from afar these tremendous subjects of 
dispute, wait blindfold the event and final decision of the 
controversy. Nor is it enough that we are merely passive. 
Tis requii'ed of us, that in the midst of this irreconcilable 
debate concerning heavenly authorities and powers, we should 
be as confident of the veracity of some one, as of the imposture 
and cheat of all the other pretenders ; and that, believing firmly 
there is still a real commission at the bottom, we should endure 
the misery of these conflicts and engage on one side or the other 
as we happen to have our birth or education, till by fire and 
sword, execution, massacre, and a kind of depopulation of this
